Hirzebruch–Zagier cycles in p p -adic families and adjoint L L -values Thanks: A.C.’s research was partly funded by the NSERC grant RGPIN-2018-04392, the FRQ-NT Team grant 2019-PR-256236, and the Concordia Horizon postdoc fellowship n.8009, by the JSPS Postdoctoral Fellowship for Research in Japan, and by Taighde Éireann – Research Ireland under Grant number IRCLA/2023/849 (HighCritical). Thanks: M.-H.N.’s research was partly funded by NSERC grant RGPIN-2019-06957. Thanks: G.R.’s research was partly funded by the NOVA-FRQNT-CRSNG grant 325940, the FRQ-NT Team grant 2019-PR-256236, and the NSERC grant RGPIN-2018-04392
Antonio Cauchi, Marc-Hubert Nicole, Giovanni Rosso

Abstract
. Let E / F E/F be a quadratic extension of totally real number fields. We show that the generalized Hirzebruch–Zagier cycles arising from the associated Hilbert modular varieties can be put in p p -adic families. As an application, using the theory of base change, we give a geometric construction of the multivariable p p -adic adjoint L L -function twisted by the Hecke character of E / F E/F , attached to Hida families of Hilbert modular forms over F F .



1.
Introduction

The theme of
p
-adic variation has irrigated much of modern arithmetic geometry in the last half-century especially through modular forms and Galois representations. In recent years, more and more examples of
p
-adic families of cohomology classes of algebraic cycles, e.g. Heegner points, have been unearthed and utilized in arithmetic applications. More generally, for a large class of Shimura varieties, their associated special cycles can be packaged compatibly in
p
-power level towers and further in
p
-adic families. In this paper, we take on the task of filling a classical gap in the literature: Hilbert modular varieties. In particular, a central technical contribution of this paper is to exhibit the
p
-adic variation of Hirzebruch–Zagier divisors and more generally so-called Hirzebruch–Zagier cycles in higher dimension.
Indeed, Hirzebruch and Zagier in their epoch-making paper showed that explicitly defined divisors (essentially, images of modular curves) on a Hilbert modular surface over
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{p})
for
p\equiv 1\mod{4}
are Fourier coefficients of a classical modular form of weight 2. A vast web of deep conjectures generalizing the Hirzebruch–Zagier theorem on Hilbert modular surfaces as well as the Gross–Kohnen–Zagier theorem on modular curves, has been developed under the banner of the Kudla program especially for orthogonal and unitary groups. Since it befits squarely our purpose, we rely on the framework of the treatise of Getz and Goresky
[
17
]
that reworked and much generalized the original Hirzebruch–Zagier theorem from Hilbert modular surfaces to higher dimensional Hilbert modular varieties, by crucially using as a central tool relative quadratic base change for automorphic representations.

1.1.
Main results

Let
E/F
be a quadratic extension of totally real number fields with
[F:{\mathbb{Q}}]=d
and let

H={\rm Res}_{\mathcal{O}_{F}/{\mathbb{Z}}}\mathrm{GL}_{2},\,\,G={\rm Res}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}/{\mathbb{Z}}}\mathrm{GL}_{2}.

There are Hilbert modular varieties
Y_{H}
and
Y_{G}
of dimension
d
and
2d
associated with
H
and
G
, respectively. The natural embedding of
H
into
G
induces an embedding
\iota:Y_{H}\hookrightarrow Y_{G}
of codimension
d
. The cycle determined by
\iota
is referred to as the Hirzebruch–Zagier cycle. Let
\Sigma_{E}
denote the set of archimedean places of
E
and
\Phi
be the
F
-type for
E
defined in (
2
). Then, using branching laws for the restriction to
H
of algebraic representations of
G
and the pushforward by
\iota
, one can construct the cycle in middle degree cohomology of
Y_{G}

\mathscr{Z}_{K}^{(\underline{k},m)}\in H^{2d}(Y_{G}(K),\mathscr{V}^{(\underline{k},m)}).

Here
K\subseteq G(\widehat{{\mathbb{Z}}})
denotes a suitable neat compact open subgroup and
\mathscr{V}^{(\underline{k},m)}
is the local system attached to the algebraic representation of
G
of highest weight
(\underline{k},m)\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0})^{\Sigma_{E}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}
s.t.
k_{\sigma}\equiv m
(mod
2
) and
k_{\sigma}=k_{\sigma^{\prime}}
for every
\sigma\in\Phi
. These cycles, or rather an analogous construction in intersection cohomology, play a crucial role in
[
17
]
. Indeed, their Hecke translates are used to construct a Fourier series which defines a Hilbert modular form on
H
with coefficients in the part of the middle degree intersection cohomology of
Y_{G}
supported by base change Hilbert modular forms from
H
. The significance of these cycles is underscored by their link with the special value at
s=1
of the adjoint
L
-function for
H
, which we now recall in a simplified form. We invite the reader to consult §
3.3
and §
7.1
for further details.

Let
g
be a cusp eigenform for
G
of level
K
, weight
(\underline{k},0)
, and trivial Nebentypus and consider its twist
g_{0}=g\otimes\theta^{-1}
with
\theta
a unitary Hecke character on
{\mathbb{A}}_{E}^{\times}
whose restriction to
{\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\times}
equals to the quadratic Hecke character
\eta_{E/F}
of
E/F
. Attached to
g_{0}
and an
F
-type
J
, there is a rapidly decreasing differential form
\omega_{J}(g_{0}^{-\iota})
, see (
4
). By an analytic formula of Hida, the Poincaré pairing between
\omega_{J}(g_{0}^{-\iota})
and the Hirzebruch–Zagier cycle
\mathscr{Z}_{K}^{(\underline{k},0)}
gives

(1)

\displaystyle\langle\omega_{J}(g_{0}^{-\iota}),\mathscr{Z}_{K}^{(\underline{k},0)}\rangle=C\cdot{\rm Res}_{s=1}L({\rm As}(g_{0}),s),

with
C
an explicit non-zero constant and
L({\rm As}(g_{0}),s)
the (partial) Asai
L
-function of
g_{0}
. Note that the non-triviality of the left hand side implies that the form
g_{0}
is
H
-distinguished, which is equivalent to
g
being a base change form on
H
. Indeed, an analysis of the possible pole at
s=1
(see
[
17
, Theorem 10.1]
) shows that
L({\rm As}(g_{0}),s)
has a (simple) pole at
s=1
if and only if
g
is the base change of a cusp form
f
on
H
with trivial Nebentypus, in which case

{\rm Res}_{s=1}L({\rm As}(g_{0}),s)=C^{\prime}\cdot L({\rm Ad}(f)\otimes\eta_{E/F},1),

where
L({\rm Ad}(f)\otimes\eta_{E/F},s)
is the (partial) adjoint
L
-function of
f
twisted by the Hecke character
\eta_{E/F}
and
C^{\prime}
is an explicit non-zero constant coming from the residue at
s=1
of a partial Dedekind zeta function. Moreover, as shown by Hida in
[
25
]
, after appropriately normalizing the differential form and cycle, the pairing in (
1
) recovers the algebraic part of the adjoint
L
-value
L({\rm Ad}(f)\otimes\eta_{E/F},1)
.

Let
p
be an odd prime which is unramified in
E
. Our main results may be summarized briefly as follows : we investigate the
p
-adic interpolation of Hirzebruch–Zagier cycles and, by exploiting the theory of base change and the integral formula (
1
), we construct a
p
-adic
L
-function that interpolates the values
L({\rm Ad}(f)\otimes\eta_{E/F},1)
as
f
varies in a Hida family of Hilbert modular forms on
H
.

Regarding our first main result, we prove the existence of a Big Hirzebruch–Zagier cycle in middle-dimensional (ordinary) Iwasawa cohomology.
To achieve this, we use the methods introduced in
[
32
]
and
[
31
]
. We would like to point out that the results in
loc. cit.
do not apply directly to our setting exactly because we work with Hilbert modular varieties that do not satisfy Milne’s axiom SV5. We however provide the necessary details to show that this hypothesis can be removed in our case.

Let
L
be a finite extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
over which
G
and
H
split and let
\mathcal{O}
be its ring of integers. As
(G,H)
is a spherical pair, we can choose
u\in G({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
such that
H({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\cdot u\cdot\overline{B}_{G}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
is dense and open in
G({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
, with
\overline{B}_{G}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
the lower-triangular Borel subgroup of
G
. Following the recipe in
[
32
, §4.4]
, from this open orbit, we define a tower of level subgroups
V_{\boldsymbol{r}}=K^{(p)}V_{\boldsymbol{r},p}
, for suitable neat open compact
K^{(p)}\subseteq G(\hat{{\mathbb{Z}}}^{(p)})
, indexed by
\boldsymbol{r}=(r_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{|\Upsilon_{F,p}|}
, where
\Upsilon_{F,p}
denotes the set of primes of
F
above
p
. Along this tower, we consider the Hecke operator
T_{\eta}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\in\Upsilon_{F,p}}T_{\eta_{\mathfrak{p}}}
, where
T_{\eta_{\mathfrak{p}}}
is the suitably normalised correspondence
[V_{\boldsymbol{r}}\eta_{\mathfrak{p}}^{-1}V_{\boldsymbol{r}}]
and
\eta_{\mathfrak{p}}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pi_{\mathfrak{p}}&\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}(F_{\mathfrak{p}})
(see Remark
4.14
). We then have the middle degree ordinary Iwasawa cohomology group

e^{\rm n.ord}H^{2d}_{\rm Iw}(Y_{G}(V_{\infty}),\mathcal{O})\cong e^{\rm n.ord}H^{2d}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(p^{2})),\Lambda_{G/H}),

where
K_{0}(p^{2})=K^{(p)}\Gamma_{0}(p^{2})
,
\Lambda_{G/H}
is the Iwasawa algebra introduced in (
19
), and
e^{\rm n.ord}
is the ordinary projector associated to
T_{\eta}
. Interestingly,
\Lambda_{G/H}
is closely related to the Iwasawa algebra
\Lambda_{H/Z_{H}}=\mathcal{O}\llbracket T_{H}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})/Z_{H}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket
, where
Z_{H}
denotes the center of
H
, and isomorphic to it if Leopoldt conjecture for
E
holds. We note that the Hecke eigensystems attached to the base change of nearly ordinary cusp forms on
H
with trivial Nebentypus contribute to
e^{\rm n.ord}H^{2d}_{\rm Iw}(Y_{G}(V_{\infty}),\mathcal{O})
.

Twisting the Hirzebruch–Zagier cycles of weight
(\underline{k},0)
with the operator
u\cdot\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\in\Upsilon_{F,p}}\eta_{\mathfrak{p}}^{r_{\mathfrak{p}}}\in G({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, we obtain cohomology classes

\mathscr{Z}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\underline{k}}\in H^{2d}(Y_{G}(V_{\boldsymbol{r}}),\mathscr{V}^{\underline{k},0}_{\mathcal{O}})

and show that they can be packaged into an Iwasawa cohomology class. Namely, we prove the following , see Theorem
5.4
and Corollary
5.7
.

Theorem 1.1
.

There is a Big Hirzebruch–Zagier class

\mathscr{Z}_{\infty}\in e^{\mathrm{n}.\mathrm{ord}}H^{2d}_{\rm Iw}(Y_{G}(V_{\infty}),\mathcal{O}).

Let
\underline{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Sigma_{E}}
be such that
k_{\sigma}\equiv 0
(mod
2
) and
k_{\sigma}=k_{\sigma^{\prime}}
for all
\sigma\in\Phi
. Then,

(1)

for every
\boldsymbol{r}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}^{|\Upsilon_{F,p}|}
, we have

\mathrm{mom}^{\underline{k}}_{\boldsymbol{r}}\,\mathscr{Z}_{\infty}=T_{\eta}^{-\boldsymbol{r}}e^{\mathrm{n}.\mathrm{ord}}\,\mathscr{Z}_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\underline{k}},

with
T_{\eta}^{-\boldsymbol{r}}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}T_{\eta_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{-r_{\mathfrak{p}}}
;

(2)

if
\boldsymbol{r}=\boldsymbol{0}
,

\mathrm{mom}^{\underline{k}}_{0}\,\mathscr{Z}_{\infty}=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\in\Upsilon_{F,p}}\left(1-q_{\mathfrak{p}}\cdot p^{\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}k_{\sigma}}\,T_{\eta_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{-1}\right)\cdot e^{\mathrm{n}.\mathrm{ord}}\mathscr{Z}_{0}^{\underline{k}}.

Our second main result is the construction of the adjoint
p
-adic
L
-function, interpolating the values
L(\mathrm{Ad}(f)\otimes\eta_{E/F},1)
as
f
varies in a nearly ordinary Hida family for
F
in
d
variables. Let
\mathbf{f}
be a Hida family of tame character
\chi_{F}
of cusp forms on
H
, and
\mathbf{f}_{E}
its base change to
E/F
of tame character
\chi=\chi_{F}\circ{\rm N}_{E/F}
, see Definition
6.9
. For a unitary Hecke character
\theta
of
{\mathbb{A}}_{E}^{\times}
with conductor coprime with
p
and such that its restriction to
{\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\times}
is
\chi_{F}\eta_{E/F}
, we consider the twist
\mathscr{Z}_{\infty,\theta}
of the Big Hirzebruch–Zagier using the correspondence of
[
17
, Proposition 9.5]
. The
p
-adic
L
-function attached to
\mathbf{f}
is defined as the image of the Big Hirzebruch–Zagier class
\mathscr{Z}_{\infty,\theta}
under a suitable idempotent associated to
\mathbf{f}_{E}
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\mathbf{f}
a Hida family of cuspidal Hilbert modular forms for
F
in the sense of Definition
6.4
and let
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{f}}
be the fraction field of the coefficient of
\mathbf{f}
. Suppose that the image of its residual Galois representation is not solvable. Then there exist a non-zero element of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{f}}
, denoted by
L_{p}(\mathrm{Ad}(\mathbf{f})\otimes\eta_{E/F})
, which interpolates the algebraic part of the special value
L(\mathrm{Ad}(f)\otimes\eta_{E/F},s)
at
s=1
.

We call
L_{p}(\mathrm{Ad}(\mathbf{f})\otimes\eta_{E/F})
the adjoint
p
-adic
L
-function twisted by
\eta_{E/F}
of
\mathbf{f}
because it satisfies the expected interpolation properties at classical weights. We refer to Theorem
7.8
for the precise statement of the interpolation formulae as well as the description of the Euler factors at
p
appearing therein.

We expect the Hirzebruch–Zagier theorem to be naturally compatible with the
p
-adic variation of Hirzebruch–Zagier divisors. As a step in that direction, in Remark
7.9
, we define an element of
e^{\mathrm{n}.\mathrm{ord}}H^{2d}_{\rm Iw}(Y_{G}(V_{\infty}),\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{m}})\otimes_{\Lambda_{G/H}}\mathcal{K}
, where
\mathcal{K}
is a large enough extension of
\mathrm{Frac}(\Lambda_{G/H})
, by taking the sum of the adjoint
p
-adic
L
-functions of each primitive base-change family contributing to the Iwasawa cohomology group. This can be seen as a family version of the element
\Phi_{Q([Z]),\chi_{E}}
of
[
17
, Theorem 8.4]
. It would be interesting to upgrade Remark
7.9
and construct a
\Lambda_{H/Z_{H}}
-adic Hilbert modular form valued in the ordinary Iwasawa cohomology group studied here. We shall come back to this in future work. Note that the
p
-adic variation of the Gross–Kohnen–Zagier theorem via Big Heegner points has been treated by Longo and Nicole in
[
33
]
, and
[
34
]
.

1.2.
Relation to previous literature

The adjoint
p
-adic
L
-functions in several variables for Hilbert modular forms and their twist interpolating the values of the adjoint
L
-function at Deligne critical values have been constructed in
[
22
,
42
,
38
]
, see also
[
37
]
for the interpolation of critical values on the right side of the center of the functional equation. Still, due to the presence of trivial zeroes, these
p
-adic
L
-functions generally vanish at the near central value
0
(or
1
).

If one removes the cyclotomic variable, than one can get rid of the trivial zeroes and interpolate the special value at
1
: in the literature, this has been done by interpreting the congruence module of a family as the adjoint
p
-adic
L
-function, see for example
[
19
,
3
]
. This is done thanks to a formula of Shimura that interprets the Petersson norm of a Hilbert modular form
f
as the algebraic part of
L(\mathrm{Ad}(f),1)
and, in turn, the congruence module can be related to the (algebraically normalised) Petersson norm.
Hence, the congruence module corresponds, up to a
p
-adic unit, with the algebraic part of
L(\mathrm{Ad}(f),1)
and thence the name of
p
-adic adjoint
L
-function for the congruence module, see also
[
12
,
18
,
1
]
for the generalisation to Hilbert modular forms.

In our paper, we construct a many-variable
p
-adic
L
-function interpolating the values
L(\mathrm{Ad}(f)\otimes\eta_{E/F},1)
, for
E
a fixed quadratic totally real extension of
F
,
\eta_{E/F}
the adelic character of
F
corresponding to this extension under class field theory, and where
f
varies in a nearly ordinary Hida family for
F
in
d
variables. We do not use an interpretation of these values as a congruence number but rather use an analytic formula of Hida
[
25
]
. Still, note that a suitable integral normalisation of these values should control congruences between the base change of
f
to
E
and non-base change forms, see
[
14
, Conjecture 1.1]
.

A rather similar approach as ours albeit without the construction of the big class in Iwasawa cohomology has been used for to construct a
p
-adic
L
-function interpolating
L(\mathrm{Ad}(f)\otimes\eta_{E/\mathbb{Q}},1)
where
E
is a quadratic imaginary field, see
[
29
]
. See also
[
15
]
where, building on ideas of Darmon and Rotger e.g.,
[
10
]
, the authors construct
p
-adic families of twisted diagonal cycles associated to the extension
(E\times{\mathbb{Q}})/{\mathbb{Q}}
, where
E
is a real quadratic field. Similar techniques apply also for other cycles and/or automorphic
L
-functions, see for example
[
4
,
31
]
.
We finally note that that the techniques introduced in
[
36
]
could be used to generalize Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
to non-ordinary situations, which we plan to explore in future work.

1.3.
Structure of the manuscript

We describe the content of the paper. In Section 2, we present some prolegomenas on algebraic representations and associated local systems with emphasis on a description via an explicit invariant vector of the branching law associated to the diagonal embedding associated to the relatively quadratic totally real field extension
E/F
. In Section 3, we recall basic notations on Hilbert modular forms and differential forms,
L
-functions, and adjoint
L
-values. In Section 4, we describe the
p
-adic tower of
p
-power level subgroups and the moment map allowing to pass from the limit in cohomology to any finite level. We conclude the section with a Control Theorem for this
p
-adic tower of level subgroups, see Theorem
4.34
. In Section 5, we consider the Hilbert modular varieties of
p
-power level specified in the previous Section. We describe the Hirzebruch–Zagier cycles arising therein, forming a Big Hirzebruch–Zagier class, see Theorem
5.4
. We also compare our cycles with the cycles of Getz and Goresky, see Theorem
5.6
. In Section 6, we recall briefly elements of Hida theory for Hilbert modular forms and define base change Hida families. In Section 7, as a first application of the existence of the
p
-adic family of Hirzebruch–Zagier cycles developed in Section 5, we construct geometrically the
p
-adic adjoint
L
-function, see Theorem
7.8
in the text.

2.
Preliminaries on algebraic representations and their local systems

Let
F
be a totally real field over
{\mathbb{Q}}
of degree
[F:{\mathbb{Q}}]=d
. Denote
\Sigma_{F}
the set of archimedean places of
F
. Let
p
be an odd prime number. We fix an isomorphism
i_{p}:{\mathbb{C}}\cong\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
, in order to identify elements in
\Sigma_{F}
with
p
-adic places eventually. We consider
E/F
a quadratic extension of totally real fields, and suppose that
E=F(\sqrt{\Delta})
, for a totally positive
\Delta\in F
. We suppose henceforth that
p
is an unramified prime in
E
. In analogy with the notion of a CM-type, we say that a subset
J
of
\Sigma_{E}
is a
F
-type for
E
if for every
\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}
there is exactly one embedding extending
\sigma
in
J
, so
\Sigma_{E}=J\sqcup\overline{J}
with respect to real conjugation in
{\rm Gal}(E/F)
. We denote by
\mathcal{O}_{E}
(resp.
\mathcal{O}_{F}
) the ring of integers of
E
(resp.
F
) and we write
D_{E}
(resp.
D_{F}
) for the (absolute) discriminant of
E
(resp.
F
) over
\mathbb{Q}
. We also write
\mathfrak{d}_{E/F}
for the discriminant of
E
over
F
, which is an ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{F}
.

Let
{\rm Gal}(E/F)=\langle\tau\rangle
. For every
\sigma\in\Sigma_{E}
, we denote the pre-composition by
\tau
by
\sigma^{\prime}:=\sigma\circ\tau
. We define the
F
-type
\Phi
for
E
as

(2)

\displaystyle\Phi:=\{\sigma\in{\rm\Sigma_{E}}\,:\,\sigma(\sqrt{\Delta})<0\}.

Then

\displaystyle{\rm\Sigma_{E}}=\Phi\sqcup\Phi^{\prime},

with
\Phi^{\prime}:=\{\sigma^{\prime}\}_{\sigma\in\Phi}
.

2.1.
Groups and varieties

Let
{\mathrm{GL}_{2}}
be the group scheme over
{\mathbb{Z}}
of invertible 2-by-2 matrices.
For
E/F
a quadratic extension of totally real fields, we let

H{\@index{\@indexphrase{$H$}}}:={\rm Res}_{\mathcal{O}_{F}/{\mathbb{Z}}}\mathrm{GL}_{2},

G{\@index{\@indexphrase{$G$}}}:={\rm Res}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}/{\mathbb{Z}}}\mathrm{GL}_{2}.

We denote by
Z_{H},Z_{G}
the centers of
H
and
G
respectively.

Note that we have the natural diagonal embedding
\iota:H\hookrightarrow G
obtained from viewing
G
as the restriction of scalars of
H
with respect to
E/F
. Now, let
L
be a field over which
H
and
G
are split. Then,
\iota:H_{L}\to G_{L}
is induced by the diagonal embedding

\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}}\mathrm{GL}_{2,L}\hookrightarrow\prod_{\sigma\in\Phi}\Big(\mathrm{GL}_{2,L}\times\mathrm{GL}_{2,L}\Big),\;(g_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\mapsto(g_{\sigma},g_{\sigma^{\prime}})_{\sigma},

where
\Phi
is the
F
-type defined by (
2
).

We denote by
Y_{H}
(resp.
Y_{G}
) the Hilbert modular variety associated with
H
(resp.
G)
. Recall that the associated Hermitian symmetric domains are the product of
[F:{\mathbb{Q}}]=d
(resp.
[E:{\mathbb{Q}}]=2d
) copies of the Poincaré upper-half plane
\mathfrak{H}
.

Definition 2.1
.

We define
K_{\infty}^{+}
to be the connected component containing the identity of the maximal compact subgroup
K_{\infty}
of
G({\mathbb{R}})
. Let
K
be a compact-open subgroup of
G({\mathbb{A}}_{f})
. We write

G({\mathbb{A}}_{f})=\bigcup_{i}G({\mathbb{Q}})t_{i}KK_{\infty}^{+}.

We say that
K
is neat if

\overline{\Gamma}_{i}(K):={\rm Im}(t_{i}KK_{\infty}^{+}t_{i}^{-1}\cap G({\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow{\rm PGL}_{2}(E))

acts without fixed points on
\mathfrak{H}^{2d}
.

An analogous definition can be made for a compact-open subgroup of
H({\mathbb{A}}_{f})
. When
K
and
K^{\prime}
are neat compact-open subgroups respectively of
G({\mathbb{A}}_{f})
and
H({\mathbb{A}}_{f})
,
Y_{G}(K)
and
Y_{H}(K^{\prime})
are smooth quasi-projective schemes over
{\rm Spec}\,{\mathbb{Q}}
of dimension
2d
and
d
respectively.

2.2.
Representations

From now on,
L
denotes a field for which
H
and
G
are split. Let
V
be the standard representation
\textbf{GL}_{2,L}
acting on the left on
V\simeq L^{2}
with given basis
(e_{1},e_{2})
, and denote
{\rm det}:=\bigwedge^{2}V
by minor abuse of notation. Every irreducible algebraic representation of
\textbf{GL}_{2,L}
is of the form
V^{k,m}:={\rm Sym}^{k}(V)\otimes{\rm det}^{m}
, with
(k,m)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\times{\mathbb{Z}}
. Here we denote by
{\rm Sym}^{k}(V)
the quotient module of co-invariants of
V^{\otimes k}
for the action of the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
on
k
elements.

Definition 2.2
.

For
(\underline{k},m)\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0})^{\Sigma_{E}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}
s.t.
k_{\sigma}\equiv m
(mod
2
), where we write
\underline{k}=(k_{\sigma})_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{E}}
, we define

V^{\underline{k},m}:=\bigotimes_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{E}}{\rm Sym}^{k_{\sigma}}(V_{\sigma})\otimes\mathrm{det}_{\sigma}^{\frac{m-k_{\sigma}}{2}}.

Remark 2.3
.

By a slight abuse of notation, we shall consider algebraic representation of
H
of weights
(\underline{k},m)\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0})^{\Sigma_{F}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}
that will be denoted by the same symbol
V^{\underline{k},m}
. The weight
\underline{k}
and the context will make clear if we are working with representations of
H
or
G
.

Remark 2.4
.

The highest weight vector of
V^{\underline{k},m}
is given by
\bigotimes_{\sigma}e_{1,\sigma}^{\otimes k_{\sigma}}
, where
(e_{1,\sigma},e_{2,\sigma})
is the standard basis of each
V_{\sigma}
.

Remark 2.5
.

The contragredient of

V^{\underline{k},m}
is isomorphic to
V^{\underline{k},-m}
.

We can realize
V^{\underline{k},m}
explicitly as follows. The
L
-vector space
L[X_{\sigma},Y_{\sigma}]^{=k_{\sigma}}
of homogeneous polynomials of degree
k_{\sigma}
is a model for the dual representation of
{\rm Sym}^{k_{\sigma}}(V_{\sigma})
. The element
g\in\textbf{GL}_{2,L}
acts on
L[X_{\sigma},Y_{\sigma}]^{=k_{\sigma}}
via the left action given by

g\cdot P(X,Y)=\mathrm{det}(g)^{-k_{\sigma}}P\left(\left(g^{*}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}X\\
Y\end{array}\right)\right)^{t}\right)=\mathrm{det}(g)^{-k_{\sigma}}P(dX-bY,-cX+aY),

where for
g=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right)
,
g^{*}=\mathrm{det}(g)g^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}d&-b\\
-c&a\end{array}\right)
. Observe that, as
\mathrm{SL}_{2,L}
-representations,
{\rm Sym}^{k_{\sigma}}(V_{\sigma})
and
L[X_{\sigma},Y_{\sigma}]^{=k_{\sigma}}
are isomorphic via sending

\alpha e_{1}+\beta e_{2}\mapsto-\beta X+\alpha Y.

This implies that
L[X_{\sigma},Y_{\sigma}]^{=k_{\sigma}}\simeq{\rm Sym}^{k_{\sigma}}(V_{\sigma})\otimes{\rm det}_{\sigma}^{-k_{\sigma}}
as
\textbf{GL}_{2,L}
-representations. Via this identification,
e_{1,\sigma}
hence
Y_{\sigma}
are the highest weight vectors for
V_{\sigma}
and its dual. This implies that the dual of
\bigotimes_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{E}}{\rm Sym}^{k_{\sigma}}(V_{\sigma})
as a
G_{L}
-representation is isomorphic to
\bigotimes_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{E}}L[X_{\sigma},Y_{\sigma}]^{=k_{\sigma}}
.

We introduce some useful lattices in
{\rm Sym}^{k}(V)
. Let
\mathcal{O}:=\mathcal{O}_{L}
be the ring of integers of
L
, and identify
\mathcal{O}^{2}
with
\mathcal{O}e_{1}\oplus\mathcal{O}e_{2}
inside
V
. We have

{\rm TSym}^{k}(\mathcal{O}^{2})\subset{\rm Sym}^{k}(\mathcal{O}^{2})\subset{\rm Sym}^{k}(V).

Recall that
{\rm Sym}^{k}(\mathcal{O}^{2})
is the module of
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
-coinvariants, and
{\rm TSym}^{k}(\mathcal{O}^{2})
is the submodule of
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
-invariants in
{(\mathcal{O}^{2})}^{\otimes k}
, that we then identify with its image in
{\rm Sym}^{k}(\mathcal{O}^{2})
. Recall that a lattice in
{\rm Sym}^{k}(V)
is
admissible
if it is invariant under the Kostant
{\mathbb{Z}}
-form in the universal enveloping algebra of the Lie algebra of
\textbf{GL}_{2,L}
. Every admissible lattice is a direct sum of its weight components, thus an admissible lattice in
{\rm Sym}^{k}(V)
is proportional to one whose intersection with the highest weight subspace is
\mathcal{O}\cdot e_{1}^{\otimes k}
. Hence, without loss of generality, we can suppose this intersection to be precisely
\mathcal{O}\cdot e_{1}^{\otimes k}
. Finally, recall that any admissible lattice is invariant under the action of
\textbf{GL}_{2,\mathcal{O}}
(for instance, see
[
30
]
for more details). Note that
{\rm Sym}^{k}(\mathcal{O}^{2})
is the maximal admissible lattice in
{\rm Sym}^{k}(V)
, while
{\rm TSym}^{k}(\mathcal{O}^{2})
is the minimal admissible one. Indeed, by duality, we have two lattices in
L[X,Y]^{=k}
:
\mathcal{O}[X,Y]^{=k}
, the set of homogeneous polynomials of degree
k
with integer coefficients, which is a maximal lattice and it is dual to
{\rm TSym}^{k}(\mathcal{O}^{2})
,
and
\mathcal{O}\left[\binom{k}{i}X^{k-i}Y^{i}\right]
, which is a minimal lattice and is dual to
{\rm Sym}^{k}(\mathcal{O}^{2})
. It coincides with the duality of
[
17
, §7.5]
(under the isomorphism above).

Given a finite order Hecke character
\chi
of
E
of conductor dividing an ideal
\mathfrak{c}
of
\mathcal{O}_{E}
, we can define a character of

K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}):=\{g\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{E})\,:\,g={{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\big)}},\text{ with }c\equiv 0\text{ mod }\mathfrak{c}\}

via

{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}\mapsto\chi(d)
and consequently a one dimensional representation
L({\chi})
of
K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})
. Following
[
17
, §6.8]
, we denote
V^{\underline{k},{m}}(\chi):=V^{\underline{k},{m}}\otimes_{L}L(\chi)
. Explicitly, for
v\in V^{\underline{k},{m}}(\chi)
and
g\in K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})
, we have
g.v=\chi(g)v
.

We conclude by defining two more representations of
G(L)
, that are not necessarily algebraic.

Definition 2.6
.

For
(\underline{k},m)\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0})^{\Sigma_{E}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}
we define the complex representation of
G(L)

V^{\underline{k},m}_{\mathbb{C}}:=\bigotimes_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{E}}{\rm Sym}^{k_{\sigma}}(V_{\sigma}\otimes{\mathbb{C}})\otimes\mathrm{det}_{\sigma}^{\frac{m-k_{\sigma}}{2}}.

If, instead, we consider a
p
-adic field
L_{\mathfrak{m}}
that contains all the square roots of elements of
E\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
(note that this is a finite extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
) and denote by
\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{m}}
its ring of integers, we define, for
(\underline{k},m)\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0})^{\Sigma_{E}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}
, the
p
-adic representation of
G(\mathcal{O})

V^{\underline{k},m}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{m}}}:=\bigotimes_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{E}}{\rm Sym}^{k_{\sigma}}(\mathcal{O}^{2}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{m}})\otimes\mathrm{det}_{\sigma}^{\frac{m-k_{\sigma}}{2}}.

2.3.
Branching laws

Recall that
\Phi
denotes the
F
-type for
E
given by (
2
) and that the embedding
\iota:H_{L}\to G_{L}
is induced by the diagonal embedding:

\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}}\mathrm{GL}_{2,L}\hookrightarrow\prod_{\sigma\in\Phi}\Big(\mathrm{GL}_{2,L}\times\mathrm{GL}_{2,L}\Big),\;(g_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\mapsto(g_{\sigma},g_{\sigma^{\prime}})_{\sigma}.

Given the
G_{L}
-representation
V^{\underline{k},m}
, we can view it as an
H_{L}
-representation via the embedding
\iota
. It decomposes into a sum of irreducible sub-
H_{L}
-representations. In the following, we characterize the set of representations of
G_{L}
which contain a trivial
H_{L}
-representation.

Lemma 2.7
.

The representation
V^{\underline{k},m}_{|_{H_{L}}}
contains a unique copy of the trivial
H_{L}
-representation (up to a twist) if and only if
k_{\sigma}=k_{\sigma^{\prime}}
for all
\sigma\in\Phi
. In which case, we have an embedding

\iota^{\underline{k}}:\bigotimes_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}}{\rm det}_{\sigma}^{m}\hookrightarrow V^{\underline{k},m},

explicitly realized by the vector

(3)

\Delta^{\underline{k}}:=\bigotimes_{\sigma\in\Phi}(e_{1,\sigma}e_{2,\sigma^{\prime}}-e_{2,\sigma}e_{1,\sigma^{\prime}})^{\otimes k_{\sigma}}\in V^{\underline{k},m}.

Proof.

Recall that we have the decomposition as
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(L)
-representations

(\operatorname{Sym}^{k}(V)\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{j}(V))_{|_{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(L)}}=\oplus_{r=0}^{{\rm min}(k,j)}\operatorname{Sym}^{k+j-2r}(V),

see
[
16
, Exercise 11.11]
.
This in turn implies that

\bigotimes_{\sigma\in\Phi}(\operatorname{Sym}^{k_{\sigma}}(V_{\sigma})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{k_{\sigma^{\prime}}}(V_{\sigma^{\prime}}))_{|_{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(L)}}=\bigotimes_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}}\bigoplus_{r_{\sigma}=0}^{{\rm min}(k_{\sigma},k_{\sigma^{\prime}})}\operatorname{Sym}^{k_{\sigma}+k_{\sigma^{\prime}}-2r_{\sigma}}(V_{\sigma}).

Hence we have an embedding of the trivial
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(L)^{d}
-representation

\mathbf{1}\hookrightarrow\bigotimes_{\sigma\in\Phi}(\operatorname{Sym}^{k_{\sigma}}(V_{\sigma})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{k_{\sigma^{\prime}}}(V_{\sigma^{\prime}}))

if and only if
k_{\sigma}=k_{\sigma^{\prime}}
for all pairs of conjugate embeddings
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
, as
k_{\sigma}+k_{\sigma^{\prime}}-{2\rm min}(k_{\sigma},k_{\sigma^{\prime}})=0
iff
k_{\sigma}=k_{\sigma^{\prime}}
for all such pairs. By matching the central characters, we get an embedding of
H_{L}
-representations

\iota^{\underline{k}}:\bigotimes_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}}{\rm det}_{\sigma}^{m}\hookrightarrow\bigotimes_{\sigma\in\Phi}(\operatorname{Sym}^{k_{\sigma}}(V_{\sigma})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{k_{\sigma}}(V_{\sigma})\otimes{\rm det}_{\sigma}^{m-k_{\sigma}})=V^{\underline{k},m}_{|_{H_{L}}}.

Finally, note that the invariant vector (
cf
.
\Delta
in
[
17
, Proposition 9.1]
) which generates the image of
\iota^{\underline{k}}
is explicitly given by:

\bigotimes_{\sigma\in\Phi}(e_{1,\sigma}e_{2,\sigma^{\prime}}-e_{2,\sigma}e_{1,\sigma^{\prime}})^{\otimes k_{\sigma}}.

Indeed, if
g={{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}\in\mathrm{GL}(V_{\sigma})
, we have that

\displaystyle(g,g)(e_{1,\sigma}e_{2,\sigma^{\prime}}-e_{2,\sigma}e_{1,\sigma^{\prime}})=

\displaystyle(ae_{1,\sigma}+ce_{2,\sigma})(be_{1,\sigma^{\prime}}+de_{2,\sigma^{\prime}})-(be_{1,\sigma}+de_{2,\sigma})(ae_{1,\sigma^{\prime}}+ce_{2,\sigma^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle abe_{1,\sigma}e_{1,\sigma^{\prime}}+cde_{2,\sigma}e_{2,\sigma^{\prime}}+ade_{1,\sigma}e_{2,\sigma^{\prime}}-(-bc)e_{2,\sigma}e_{1,\sigma^{\prime}}+

\displaystyle-abe_{1,\sigma}e_{1,\sigma^{\prime}}-cde_{2,\sigma}e_{2,\sigma^{\prime}}-bce_{1,\sigma}e_{2,\sigma^{\prime}}-ade_{2,\sigma}e_{1,\sigma^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\mathrm{det}(g)(e_{1,\sigma}e_{2,\sigma^{\prime}}-e_{2,\sigma}e_{1,\sigma^{\prime}}).

∎

Remark 2.8
.

Lemma
2.7
can be rephrased by saying that

{\rm dim}(V^{\underline{k},m}\otimes\bigotimes_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}}{\rm det}_{\sigma}^{-m})^{H}\leq 1

and it is equal to 1 if and only if
k_{\sigma}=k_{\sigma^{\prime}}
for every
\sigma\in\Phi
.

Lemma 2.9
.

Suppose that
k_{\sigma}=k_{\sigma^{\prime}}
for every
\sigma\in\Phi
. Then
\iota^{\underline{k}}
sends the
\mathcal{O}
-span of
\Delta^{\underline{k}}
inside an admissible lattice of
V^{\underline{k},m}
and it generates the
H
-invariants of this lattice.

Proof.

Expanding
\Delta^{\underline{k}}
, we get

\Delta^{\underline{k}}=\bigotimes_{\sigma\in\Phi}\sum_{i=0}^{k_{\sigma}}(-1)^{i}\binom{k_{\sigma}}{i}e_{1,\sigma}^{\otimes^{k_{\sigma}-i}}e_{2,\sigma}^{\otimes^{i}}e_{1,\sigma^{\prime}}^{\otimes^{i}}e_{2,\sigma^{\prime}}^{k_{\sigma}-i}

which is a vector in
\left(\bigotimes_{\sigma\in\Phi}\mathrm{Sym}^{k_{\sigma}}(\mathcal{O}^{2})\otimes\mathrm{det}_{\sigma}^{\frac{m-k_{\sigma}}{2}}\right)\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Phi^{\prime}}\mathrm{Sym}^{k_{\sigma^{\prime}}}(\mathcal{O}^{2})\otimes\mathrm{det}_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\frac{m-k_{\sigma^{\prime}}}{2}}\right)
.
∎

Remark 2.10
.

Note that in general
\Delta^{\underline{k}}
is not in the minimal admissible lattice

\bigotimes_{\sigma}\left(\mathrm{TSym}^{k_{\sigma}}(\mathcal{O}^{2})\otimes\mathrm{det}_{\sigma}^{\tfrac{m-k_{\sigma}}{2}}\right)\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{\sigma^{\prime}}\mathrm{TSym}^{k_{\sigma^{\prime}}}(\mathcal{O}^{2})\otimes\mathrm{det}_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\tfrac{m-k_{\sigma^{\prime}}}{2}}\right).

2.4.
Cohomology

Associated with the irreducible algebraic representation
V^{\underline{k},{m}}
and its maximal admissible lattice
V^{\underline{k},{m}}_{\mathcal{O}}
, we have two local systems
\mathscr{V}^{\underline{k},{m}}
and
\mathscr{V}^{\underline{k},{m}}_{\mathcal{O}}
on
Y_{G}(K)
, which can be defined as in
[
17
, §6.8]
, as long as
K\subset G({\mathbb{A}}_{f})
is a neat open compact subgroup. Similarly, by
[
17
, Proposition 6.3]
, we can define local systems
1
1

1

In
loc.cit.
, the authors use a different normalization for the weights of the algebraic representations of
G
. They write the weights as
\kappa=(\underline{t},\underline{s})\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0})^{\Sigma_{E}}\times(\tfrac{1}{2}{\mathbb{Z}})^{\Sigma_{E}}
such that
\underline{t}+2\underline{s}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{1}
. Translating from one convention to the other, our representation
V^{\underline{k},{m}}
corresponds to the dual of
L(\kappa)
, where, in the notation of
loc.cit.
,
\kappa=(\underline{t},\underline{s})
is given by
\underline{t}=\underline{k}
and
\underline{s}=\left(\frac{m-k_{\sigma}}{2}\right)_{\sigma}
. Similarly,
V^{\underline{k},{m}}(\chi)
is the dual of
L(\kappa,\chi)
. Thus, the local system
\mathcal{L}(\kappa,\chi)
of
loc.cit.
coincides with our
\mathscr{V}^{\underline{k},{-m}}(\chi^{-1})
.

\mathscr{V}^{\underline{k},{m}}(\chi)
on
Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}))
attached to a finite order Hecke character
\chi
of
E
of conductor dividing
\mathfrak{c}
and the representation
V^{\underline{k},{m}}(\chi)
of
K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})
. Similarly, we can define local systems over
Y_{H}(K^{\prime})
.

Let
\underline{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Sigma_{E}}
be such that
k_{\sigma}=k_{\sigma^{\prime}}
for all
\sigma\in\Phi
, then the branching map
\iota^{\underline{k}}_{\mathcal{O}}:\mathcal{O}\hookrightarrow V^{\underline{k},0}_{\mathcal{O}}
induces a map of local systems

\iota^{\underline{k}}_{\mathcal{O}}:\mathcal{O}\to\iota^{*}\mathscr{V}^{\underline{k},0}_{\mathcal{O}}.

Twisting the vector
\Delta^{\underline{k}}
by the finite order Hecke character
\chi
as in
[
17
, (9.2.5)]
, we can also define a map of local systems

\tilde{\iota}^{\underline{k}}_{\mathcal{O}_{\chi}}:\mathcal{O}_{\chi}\to\iota^{*}\mathscr{V}^{\underline{k},0}_{\mathcal{O}_{\chi}}(\chi),

where
\mathcal{O}_{\chi}
is the extension of
\mathcal{O}
containing the values of
\chi
.

3.
Preliminaries on Hilbert modular forms and their
L
-functions

3.1.
Hilbert modular forms and their differential forms

In this section we quickly recall some notation for Hilbert modular forms, following mainly
[
17
, §5]
.

3.1.1.
Hilbert cusp forms and their differential forms

Let
F
be a totally real field of degree
d
over
\mathbb{Q}
and let
E=F(\sqrt{\Delta})
, for
\Delta
a totally positive element of
F
.
Consider a weight
\kappa=(\underline{k},m)\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0})^{\Sigma_{E}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}
.
For an ideal
\mathfrak{c}
of
\mathcal{O}_{E}
, we consider the compact-open subgroup

K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})=\{g\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{E})\,:\,g={{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\big)}},\text{ with }c\equiv 0\text{ mod }\mathfrak{c}\}.

Let
\chi
be a Hecke character for
E
of conductor dividing
\mathfrak{c}
, such that its infinity type is given by
\chi_{\infty}(x)=\prod_{v\in\Sigma_{E}}x_{v}^{-m}
.
The space of cusp forms of weight
\kappa
and Nebentypus
\chi
is defined in
[
17
, (5.4.3)]
and as in
loc. cit.
we denote it by
2
2

2

As in the previous footnote, we observe that our weight
\kappa
corresponds to the weight
(k_{\sigma},\left(\frac{m-k_{\sigma}}{2}\right))_{\sigma}
of
[
17
]
.

S_{\kappa}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}),\chi)
. We shall denote by
f
an element of
S_{\kappa}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}),\chi)
. It corresponds to a collection of classical Hilbert modular forms defined as functions on the product of upper-half planes. Note that, following
loc. cit.
our normalization on the weight is shifted by
2
compared to the most common normalisation for Hilbert modular forms as functions on the upper half-plane. For example, we associate elliptic modular forms of weight
0
to elliptic curves over
\mathbb{Q}
.

There is also a cohomological normalization, introduced in §5.5 of
loc. cit.
, given by

f\mapsto f^{-\iota}:x\mapsto f(x^{-\iota})

for
x^{\iota}=\mathrm{det}(x)x^{-1}
and
x^{-\iota}=\mathrm{det}(x)^{-1}x
. This changes
m
to
-m
(and
\chi
to
\chi^{-1}
). This different normalization is more natural when we associate differential forms and cohomology classes to elements of
S_{\kappa}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}),\chi)
.
Precisely, denote by
\chi_{0}
the character of
K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})
, which at each finite place
v
is defined by

\chi_{v}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\begin{cases}\chi_{v}(d)&\text{ if }\mathfrak{p}_{v}\mid\mathfrak{c}\\
1&\text{ otherwise,}\end{cases}

with
\mathfrak{p}_{v}
denoting the prime ideal associated with
v
. Then, for any subset
J\subset\Sigma_{E}
, we have a map (Definition 6.8 of
loc. cit.
)

S_{\kappa}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}),\chi)\to\Omega^{2d}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}^{\underline{k},{-m}}(\chi^{-1}_{0})_{\mathbb{C}}),\,f\mapsto\omega_{J}(f^{-\iota}),

which defines a class
[\omega_{J}(f^{-\iota})]
in
H^{2d}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}^{\underline{k},{-m}}(\chi^{-1}_{0})_{\mathbb{C}})
. The map is Hecke equivariant in the sense of Proposition 7.13 of
loc.cit.
(see Lemma
6.1
below). The differential form is anti-holomorphic at places in
J
and holomorphic at places outside
J
and it is square integrable (Proposition 6.8 of
loc. cit.
), so that
[\omega_{J}(f^{-\iota})]
defines a class in
L^{2}
-cohomology of
Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}))
. Note that as
f
is a cusp form, the differential form
\omega_{J}(f^{-\iota})
is rapidly decreasing, thus

(4)

\displaystyle[\omega_{J}(f^{-\iota})]\in H^{2d}_{{\rm rd}}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}^{\underline{k},{-m}}(\chi^{-1}_{0})_{\mathbb{C}}).

Recall that such a class can be represented by a compactly supported differential form:

Proposition 3.1

(
[
6
, Corollary 5.5]
)
.

Let
f\in S_{\kappa}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}),\chi)
. There exists a
\mathscr{V}^{\underline{k},{-m}}(\chi^{-1}_{0})_{\mathbb{C}}
-valued rapidly decreasing form
\Psi
on
Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}))
such that
\omega_{c}:=\omega_{J}(f^{-\iota})+d\Psi
is compactly supported. In other words,

[\omega_{c}]=[\omega_{J}(f^{-\iota})]\in H^{2d}_{{\rm rd}}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}^{\underline{k},{-m}}(\chi^{-1}_{0})_{\mathbb{C}}).

Similar normalization and definitions can be done for Hilbert modular forms for
F
.

3.1.2.
The Fourier expansion

We recall briefly the Fourier expansion of a form
f
over
E
, following
[
17
, §5.9]
.
We define an additive character
e_{E}(x)
, for
x\in\mathbb{A}_{E}/E
. For a finite place
v
of
E
, lying above
q\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define

\begin{array}[]{cccc}e_{v}:&E_{v}&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{C}^{\times}\\
&x&\mapsto&\exp(-2\pi i\mathrm{tr}_{E_{v}}^{\mathbb{Q}_{q}}(x))\end{array}

where we identify
\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
with
\mathbb{Q}_{q}/\mathbb{Z}_{q}
.
If
v
is an archimedean place, we set
e_{v}(x):=\exp(2\pi ix)
.
We can finally define

e_{E}(x):=\prod_{v}e_{v}(x_{v}),x=(x_{v}).

If
f
is of weight
\kappa=(\underline{k},m)\in({\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0})^{\Sigma_{E}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}
, for
\sigma\in\Sigma_{E}
define

\begin{array}[]{cccc}W_{\sigma}:&\mathbb{R}^{\times}&\rightarrow&\mathbb{C}\\
&y&\mapsto&|y|^{\frac{k_{\sigma}-m}{2}}e^{-2\pi|y|},\end{array}

and finally, for
x\in\mathbb{A}_{E}
and
y\in\mathbb{A}_{E}^{\times}

q_{\kappa}(x,y):=e_{E}(x)\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{E}}W_{\sigma}(y_{\sigma}).

(Recall that our weight normalization is different from
[
17
]
.)
We let
\mathrm{diff}_{E/\mathbb{Q}}
be the different of
E
over
\mathbb{Q}
.
A modular form
f\in S_{\kappa}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}),\chi)
has a Fourier expansion of the form

(5)

f\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y&x\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)=|y|_{\mathbb{A}_{E}}\sum_{\xi\in E^{\times},\xi\gg 0}a(\xi y\mathrm{diff}_{E/\mathbb{Q}},f)q_{\kappa}(\xi x,\xi y).

When
\mathfrak{n}=(\xi y_{f}\mathcal{O}_{E})\mathrm{diff}_{E/\mathbb{Q}}
is an integral ideal, we write

a(\mathfrak{n},f)=a(\xi y\mathrm{diff}_{E/\mathbb{Q}},f).

Similar results hold for forms over
F
.

3.1.3.
The Hecke algebra

We now define the Hecke algebra for
E
, following
[
21
, §1]
. Denote

K_{11}(\mathfrak{c}):=\{g\in K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})\,:\,g\equiv{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\star\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}\text{ mod }\mathfrak{c}\}.

Definition 3.2
.

Fix a level
K\supset K_{11}(\mathfrak{c})
. For a prime ideal
\mathfrak{q}
coprime with
\mathfrak{c}
and a positive integer
l
we define the Hecke operators
T_{\mathfrak{q}^{l}},S_{\mathfrak{q}^{l}}
as the double coset operators

T_{\mathfrak{q}^{l}}:=\left[K\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\varpi_{\mathfrak{q}^{l}}&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)K\right],\quad S_{\mathfrak{q}^{l}}:=\left[K\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\varpi_{\mathfrak{q}^{l}}&0\\
0&\varpi_{\mathfrak{q}^{l}}\end{array}\right)K\right],

for
\varpi_{\mathfrak{q}}
a uniformizer at
\mathfrak{q}
. Note that as
\mathfrak{c}
is coprime to
\mathfrak{q}
, our definition does not depend on the choice of
\varpi_{\mathfrak{q}}
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be an extension of
E
containing the Galois closure of
E
and such that all the ideals of
E
are principal in
\mathcal{R}
; let
\mathcal{V}
its ring of integers. For any
\mathcal{V}
-algebra
R
we define the spherical Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}^{\rm sph}_{K,R}
of level
K
and coefficients in
R
as the
R
-algebra generated by the operators
T_{\mathfrak{q}^{l}}
and
S_{\mathfrak{q}^{l}}
as
l
and
\mathfrak{q}\nmid\mathfrak{c}
vary. In
\mathcal{H}^{\rm sph}_{K,R}
, we have the relation

T_{\mathfrak{q}}^{2}-T_{\mathfrak{q}^{2}}=\mathrm{N}_{E/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathfrak{q})S_{\mathfrak{q}}.

We define similarly
U_{\mathfrak{q}^{l}}
for
{\mathfrak{q}}
dividing
\mathfrak{c}
; but note that it could depend on the choice of uniformizer
\varpi_{\mathfrak{q}}
, see the explicit formula in
[
23
, (2.2b)]
. If
p
is coprime with
\mathfrak{q}
, then there is no need of the normalization factor
\left\{\phantom{e}\right\}^{-v}
of
[
21
, p. 142]
. We shall define later the proper normalization when
\mathfrak{q}
divides
p
. We define by
\mathcal{H}_{K,R}
the abstract Hecke algebra over
R
generated by
U_{\mathfrak{q}^{l}}
, for all
\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{c}
and
l
, and
T_{\mathfrak{q}^{l}}
,
S_{\mathfrak{q}^{l}}
for all
\mathfrak{q}\nmid\mathfrak{c}
and any
l
.

This Hecke algebra acts naturally on the space of Hilbert modular forms
[
23
, §2]
or on cohomology of Hilbert modular varieties
[
17
, §7.6]
and these actions are compatible (in the sense of
[
17
, Proposition 7.13]
).

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\chi^{\prime}
be another character of conductor
\mathfrak{c}
. We see it as a character of
K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})
via

\chi^{\prime}_{v}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\begin{cases}\chi^{\prime}_{v}(a^{-1}d)&\text{ if }\mathfrak{p}_{v}\mid\mathfrak{c}\\
1&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}.

We say that
f\in S_{\kappa}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}),\chi,\chi^{\prime})
if
f
is a cusp form of level
K_{11}(\mathfrak{c})
such that

f|\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\chi(d)\chi^{\prime}(a^{-1}d)f

for all
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})
, where we use the convention of
[
23
]
for the slash operator
|
.
We shall call
\chi
the Nebentypus of
f
and
\chi^{\prime}
its adjoint character (because it factors via the adjoint group).

Definition 3.4
.

If
f\in S_{\kappa}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}),\chi,\chi^{\prime})
is an eigenform for all but finitely many Hecke operators, we denote by
\pi(f)
the corresponding irreducible cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{E})
of central character
\chi
, as in
[
17
, (5.4.3)]
. Note that
\pi(f)
is not unitary in general, while its twist
\pi(f)\otimes|-|_{\mathbb{A}_{E}}^{m/2}
is.

Similar definitions apply to Hilbert modular forms for
F
.

3.1.4.
Atkin–Lehner operators

We recall here the Atkin–Lehner involution, following
[
17
, §7.4]
.

Choose a finite idèle
\tilde{c}
such that it generates the ideal
\mathfrak{c}
.
The Atkin–Lehner operator
W^{*}_{\mathfrak{c}}
on
S_{\kappa}(K_{1}(\mathfrak{c}))
is induced by right multiplication by the matrix

W_{\mathfrak{c}}={\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-1\\
\tilde{c}&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)}.

More precisely, it maps

f\in S_{(\underline{k},m)}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}),\chi)
to
W^{*}_{\mathfrak{c}}(f)\in S_{(\underline{k},m)}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}),\psi)
,
where

W^{*}_{\mathfrak{c}}(f)(\alpha)=\frac{|\chi(\mathrm{det}(\alpha))|}{\chi(\mathrm{det}(\alpha))}f(\alpha W_{\mathfrak{c}}),

and
\psi=\chi^{-1}|\phantom{e}|^{-2m}
(so at infinity it coincides with
\chi
, but the finite part is the inverse). Similarly, right multiplication by
W_{\mathfrak{c}}
on
Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}))
induces an isomorphism of local systems
\mathscr{V}^{\underline{k},{-m}}(\chi^{-1}_{0})\simeq\mathscr{V}^{\underline{k},{-m}}(\chi_{0})
which can be twisted to get a map on differential forms (
cf
.
[
17
, Proposition 7.6]
)

W^{*}_{\mathfrak{c}}:\Omega^{2d}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}^{\underline{k},{-m}}(\chi^{-1}_{0})_{\mathbb{C}})\to\Omega^{2d}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}^{\underline{k},{-m}}(\chi_{0})_{\mathbb{C}}),

so that we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{S_{\kappa}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}),\chi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f\mapsto\omega_{J}(f^{-\iota})}
\scriptstyle{W_{\mathfrak{c}}^{*}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{2d}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}^{\underline{k},{-m}}(\chi^{-1}_{0})_{\mathbb{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{W_{\mathfrak{c}}^{*}}
\textstyle{S_{\kappa}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}),\psi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f\mapsto\omega_{J}(f^{-\iota})}
\textstyle{\Omega^{2d}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}^{\underline{k},-m}(\chi_{0})_{\mathbb{C}}).}

Note that
W^{*}_{\mathfrak{c}}
induces an isomorphism of the Hecke algebra of level
K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})
by mapping every Hecke operator
T
to its adjoint
T^{*}
(see the proof of
[
23
, Lemma 9.3]
), namely

T^{*}=W_{\mathfrak{c},*}\circ T\circ{W^{*}_{\mathfrak{c}}}.

Remark 3.5
.

When
T=T_{\mathfrak{q}}
for
\mathfrak{q}
coprime with
\mathfrak{c}
, then
T_{\mathfrak{q}}=T_{\mathfrak{q}}^{*}
.

3.2.
L
-functions and base change

3.2.1.
The adjoint
L
-function

Let
\mathfrak{c}_{F}
be an ideal of
F
and
f
be an Hilbert cuspidal eigenform of level
K_{11}(\mathfrak{c}_{F})
, weight
(\underline{k},m)
and Nebentypus
\chi
. We assume that the form is minimal,
i.e.
that it has minimal conductor among all quadratic twists. For any Hecke character
\epsilon:F^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}^{\times}_{F}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
of conductor
\mathfrak{f}(\epsilon)
, we define the (imprimitive) adjoint
L
-function of
f
twisted by
\epsilon
as

L(\mathrm{Ad}(f)\otimes\epsilon,s):=\prod_{\mathfrak{q}\not\mid\mathfrak{f}(\epsilon)}{L_{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{Ad}(f)\otimes\epsilon,s)}.

If
\mathfrak{q}
does not divide
\mathfrak{c}_{F}
then
{L_{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{Ad}(f)\otimes\epsilon,s)}^{-1}
is defined as

\left(1-\epsilon(\mathfrak{q})\frac{\alpha_{1,\mathfrak{q}}}{\alpha_{2,\mathfrak{q}}}\mathrm{N}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathfrak{q})^{-s}\right)\left(1-\epsilon(\mathfrak{q})\mathrm{N}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathfrak{q})^{-s}\right)\left(1-\epsilon(\mathfrak{q})\frac{\alpha_{2,\mathfrak{q}}}{\alpha_{1,\mathfrak{q}}}\mathrm{N}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathfrak{q})^{-s}\right)

for
{\alpha_{1,\mathfrak{q}}},{\alpha_{2,\mathfrak{q}}}
the two Satake parameters of
\pi(f)_{\mathfrak{q}}
defined via
3
3

3

Note that we normalize the Satake parameters as in
[
17
, (5.9.6)]
, where a shift
s\mapsto s+\frac{1}{2}
from the usual normalisation is done when defining the standard
L
-function for
f
.

1-a(\mathfrak{q},f)\mathrm{N}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathfrak{q})^{-s-1/2}+\chi(\mathfrak{q})\mathrm{N}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathfrak{q})^{-2s}=(1-{\alpha_{1,\mathfrak{q}}}\mathrm{N}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathfrak{q})^{-s})(1-{\alpha_{2,\mathfrak{q}}}\mathrm{N}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathfrak{q})^{-s}).

Note that the adjoint
L
-function is independent of the variable
m
.

If
\mathfrak{q}
divides
\mathfrak{c}_{F}
but not
\mathfrak{f}(\epsilon)
, we consider the following cases.
If the local automorphic representation
\pi(f)_{\mathfrak{q}}
associated with
f
is a ramified principal series,
then
{L_{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{Ad}(f)\otimes\epsilon,s)}^{-1}
is defined as

(1-\epsilon(\mathfrak{q})\mathrm{N}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathfrak{q})^{-s}).

If
\pi(f)_{\mathfrak{q}}
is special, (
i.e.
Steinberg), then

{L_{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{Ad}(f)\otimes\epsilon,s)}^{-1}
is defined as

(1-\epsilon(\mathfrak{q})\mathrm{N}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathfrak{q})^{-1-s}).

If
\pi(f)_{\mathfrak{q}}
is supercuspidal, we set the local
L
-factor to be equal to
1
, even if the completed
L
-function could have a Euler factor different from
1
at
\mathfrak{q}
.

For
\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}
we define

{L_{\sigma}(\mathrm{Ad}(f)\otimes\epsilon,s)}:={(2\pi)}^{-(s+k_{\sigma}+1)}\Gamma(s+k_{\sigma}+1)\pi^{-(s+1)/2}\Gamma((s+1)/2).

For any other ideal
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{F}
in
\mathcal{O}_{F}
we define

L^{\ast,\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{F}}(\mathrm{Ad}(f)\otimes\epsilon,s):=\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}}L_{\sigma}(\mathrm{Ad}(f)\otimes\epsilon,s)\prod_{\mathfrak{q}\not\mid\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{F}\mathfrak{f}(\epsilon)}{L_{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{Ad}(f)\otimes\epsilon,s)}.

Finally, note that one can also define the (partial) symmetric square
L
-function for
f
twisted by
\epsilon
:

L^{\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{f}(\epsilon)}(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(f)\otimes\epsilon,s):=\prod_{\mathfrak{q}\not\mid\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{f}(\epsilon)}{L_{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(f)\otimes\epsilon,s)},

where
{L_{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(f)\otimes\epsilon,s)}^{-1}
is given by

\left(1-\epsilon(\mathfrak{q})\alpha_{1,\mathfrak{q}}^{2}\mathrm{N}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathfrak{q})^{-s}\right)\left(1-\epsilon(\mathfrak{q})\chi(\mathfrak{q})\mathrm{N}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathfrak{q})^{-s}\right)\left(1-\epsilon(\mathfrak{q})\alpha_{2,\mathfrak{q}}^{2}\mathrm{N}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathfrak{q})^{-s}\right).

Note that at any prime ideal
\mathfrak{q}
, unramified for
f
and
\chi
, we have

{L_{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(f)\otimes\chi^{-1},s)}={L_{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{Ad}(f),s)}.

3.2.2.
The Asai
L
-function

Here, we follow
[
17
, §5.12.4]
. Let
\mathfrak{c}
be an ideal of
E
, let
\chi_{F}
be a Hecke character of
{\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\times}
, and
\chi:=\chi_{F}\circ{\rm N}_{E/F}
be the corresponding Hecke character of
{\mathbb{A}}_{E}^{\times}
.
We also let
\eta_{E/F}
be the only non-trivial (quadratic) character of the Galois group of
E
over
F
, and, by abuse of notation, we use
\eta_{E/F}
to denote the corresponding Hecke character of
{\mathbb{A}}^{\times}_{F}
, trivial at the archimedean places. Recall that there is a distinguished
F
-type for
E
that we denoted by
\Phi
in §
2
. Then for a given weight
\kappa=(\underline{k},m)
for
F
, we have the corresponding weight for
E
:
\hat{\kappa}=(\hat{\underline{k}},m)
, with
\hat{k}_{\sigma}=\hat{k}_{\sigma^{\prime}}
for every
\sigma\in\Phi
.

To any Hilbert cuspidal eigenform
g
of level
K_{11}(\mathfrak{c})
, weight
\hat{\kappa}
and Nebentypus
\chi
and to any quasi-character
\theta:E^{\times}\backslash{\mathbb{A}}_{E}^{\times}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
with conductor
\mathfrak{f}(\theta)
, we can associate the Asai
L
-function of
g
twisted by
\theta
as follows
4
4

4

We again follow the normalization of
[
17
]
of the Satake parameters, which produces the shift
s\mapsto s+1
.

(11)

L(\mathrm{As}(g\otimes\theta),s):=L^{\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{f}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{O}_{F}}(\theta^{2}\chi)|_{F},2s)\sum_{\mathfrak{n}+\mathfrak{f}(\theta)=\mathcal{O}_{F}}\theta(\mathfrak{n}\mathcal{O}_{E})a(\mathfrak{n}\mathcal{O}_{E},g)\mathrm{N}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathfrak{n})^{-1-s}.

The corresponding partial Asai
L
-function of
g
admits an Euler product

L^{\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{f}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{O}_{F}}(\mathrm{As}(g\otimes\theta),s):=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\not\mid\mathfrak{c}\cap\mathcal{O}_{F}}{L_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathrm{As}(g\otimes\theta),s)},

where, if
\mathfrak{p}\nmid\mathfrak{d}_{E/F}(\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{f}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{O}_{F})
, define

L_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathrm{As}(g\otimes\theta),s)^{-1}=\begin{cases}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{2}(1-\theta(\mathfrak{p})\alpha_{i,\mathfrak{P}}\alpha_{j,\overline{\mathfrak{P}}}\mathrm{N}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathfrak{p})^{-s})&\text{if }\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{P}\overline{\mathfrak{P}}\text{ is split,}\\
(1-\theta(\mathfrak{p})^{2}\chi(\mathfrak{p})\mathrm{N}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathfrak{p})^{-2s})\prod_{i=1}^{2}(1-\theta(\mathfrak{p})\alpha_{i,\mathfrak{p}}\mathrm{N}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathfrak{p})^{-s})&\text{if }\mathfrak{p}\text{ is inert,}\end{cases}

with
\alpha_{1,\mathfrak{q}},\alpha_{2,\mathfrak{q}}
denoting the Satake parameters of
\pi(g)_{\mathfrak{q}}
(
cf
.
[
17
, §5.12.4]
).

3.2.3.
Base change forms and their
L
-function

The main reference for this section is
[
17
, Appendix E]
. If we let
G_{1}=\mathrm{GL}_{2}/F
and
G_{2}={\rm Res}_{E/F}\mathrm{GL}_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle{}^{L}G_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})\times{\rm Gal}(E/F)

\displaystyle{}^{L}G_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})^{{\rm Gal}(E/F)}\rtimes{\rm Gal}(E/F),

where in the second
{\rm Gal}(E/F)
acts on
\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})^{{\rm Gal}(E/F)}
by permuting the factors. The base change lifting from
G_{1}
to
G_{2}
is induced by the map of
L
-groups

b:{}^{L}G_{1}\to{}^{L}G_{2},

given by the diagonal embedding on the
\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
factor and by the identity on the
{\rm Gal}(E/F)
factor. For any place
v
of
F
, the map
b:{}^{L}G_{1,F_{v}}\to{}^{L}G_{2,F_{v}}
of
L
-groups over
F_{v}
is defined similarly. Suppose that
v
is a finite place of
F
which is unramified for
E/F
. Then
b
induces a morphism of local spherical Hecke algebras

b:\mathcal{H}(\mathrm{GL}_{2}(E_{v})//\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{E_{v}}))\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}(\mathrm{GL}_{2}(F_{v})//\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{F_{v}})),

where we have denoted
E_{v}=E\otimes_{F}F_{v}
. If
\pi_{v}
is an irreducible unramified admissible representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(F_{v})
, where
\mathcal{H}(\mathrm{GL}_{2}(F_{v})//\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{F_{v}}))
acts via the character
\lambda_{\pi_{v}}
, then an irreducible admissible representation
\Pi_{v}
of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(E_{v})
is the base change of
\pi_{v}
if it is unramified and

\mathcal{H}(\mathrm{GL}_{2}(E_{v})//\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{E_{v}}))

acts through the character

\lambda_{\Pi_{v}}=\lambda_{\pi_{v}}\circ b.

Concretely, if
\pi_{v}
is the principal series attached to unramified characters
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}
of
F_{v}^{\times}
,
\Pi_{v}
is the base change of
\pi_{v}
if it is the principal series of characters
\chi_{1}\circ{\rm N}_{E_{v}/F_{v}},\chi_{2}\circ{\rm N}_{E_{v}/F_{v}}
. Globally, we say that an automorphic representation
\Pi
of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{E})
is a weak base change of an automorphic representation
\pi
of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F})
if
\Pi_{v}
is the base change of
\pi_{v}
almost everywhere. Note that, by strong multiplicity for
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
, if there exists a weak base change
\Pi
of
\pi
, then it is unique.

Theorem 3.6

(
[
17
, Theorem E.11]
)
.

Every automorphic representation
\pi
of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F})
has a unique base change lift to an automorphic representation
\widehat{\pi}
of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{E})
. Moreover, any cuspidal automorphic representation
\Pi
of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{E})
that is isomorphic to its conjugates under the action of
{\rm Gal}(E/F)
is the base change lift of a cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F})
. The assignment
\pi\mapsto\widehat{\pi}
satisfies the following : If
\pi
and
\widehat{\pi}
are both cuspidal, then

(1)

If
\widehat{\pi}
is also the base change lift of another automorphic representation
\pi^{\prime}
, then
\pi^{\prime}\simeq\pi\otimes\eta_{E/F}
;

(2)

For every place
v
of
F
, then
L(\widehat{\pi}_{v},s)=L(\pi_{v},s)L(\pi_{v}\otimes\eta_{E_{v}/F_{v}},s)
.

Fix a weight
\kappa=(\underline{k},m)
for
F
and consider the corresponding weight
\hat{\kappa}=(\hat{\underline{k}},m)
for
E
. The theorem above can be used to prove the following Proposition, which we will use repeatedly in the course of the manuscript.

Proposition 3.7
.

Suppose that a newform
g\in S_{\hat{\kappa}}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}),\chi)
is an eigenform for all but finitely many Hecke operators for which the eigenvalues of
T_{\mathfrak{q}}
and
T_{\mathfrak{q}^{\sigma}}
, with
\sigma
generator of
{\rm Gal}(E/F)
, agree for almost all prime ideals
\mathfrak{q}\subset\mathcal{O}_{E}
. Then there exists an ideal
\mathfrak{c}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{F}
and a newform
f\in S_{\kappa}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}_{1}),\chi_{F})
such that
\widehat{\pi(f)}=\pi(g)
. Conversely, if
f\in S_{\kappa}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}_{F}),\chi_{F})
is a newform, there exists a unique newform
f_{E}\in S_{\hat{\kappa}}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}),\chi)
such that
\pi(f_{E})=\widehat{\pi(f)}
. Moreover, if
\theta:=\theta^{u}|\phantom{ee}|_{\mathbb{A}_{E}}^{m/2}
is a Hecke character of
\mathbb{A}^{\times}_{E}
, with
\theta^{u}
unitary, such that its restriction to
\mathbb{A}^{\times}_{F}
is
\chi_{F}\eta_{E/F}
, then

L^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{As}(f_{E}\otimes\theta^{-1}),s)=\zeta_{F}^{\mathfrak{d}_{E/F}(\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{f}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{O}_{F})}(s)L^{\mathfrak{d}_{E/F}(\mathfrak{f}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{O}_{F})}(\mathrm{Ad}(f)\otimes\eta_{E/F},s),

with
\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{p}|\mathfrak{d}_{E/F}\\
\mathfrak{p}\nmid\mathfrak{f}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{O}_{F}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{p}
. Hence,

{\rm Res}_{s=1}L^{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{As}(f_{E}\otimes\theta^{-1}),s)=L^{\mathfrak{d}_{E/F}(\mathfrak{f}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{O}_{F})}(\mathrm{Ad}(f)\otimes\eta_{E/F},1){\rm Res}_{s=1}\zeta_{F}^{\mathfrak{d}_{E/F}(\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{f}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{O}_{F})}(s).

Proof.

This follows from Corollary E.12 and Proposition 5.17 of
[
17
]
.
∎

3.3.
A cohomological formula for adjoint
L
-values

Let
\mathfrak{c}_{F}
be an ideal of
F
and
f
be a Hilbert cuspidal eigenform of level
K_{11}(\mathfrak{c}_{F})
, weight
\kappa=(\underline{k},m)
and Nebentypus
\chi_{F}
.
Let
\mathfrak{c}
be an ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{E}
, whose intersection with
\mathcal{O}_{F}
is
\mathfrak{c}_{F}
. We consider a Hecke character
\theta:=\theta^{u}|\phantom{ee}|_{\mathbb{A}_{E}}^{m/2}
of
\mathbb{A}^{\times}_{E}
such that its restriction to
\mathbb{A}^{\times}_{F}
is
\chi_{F}\eta_{E/F}
. Here
\theta^{u}
denotes the unitary part of
\theta
and
m
is as in the weight
(\underline{k},m)
. Let
\mathfrak{b}
be the conductor of
\theta^{u}
and
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{c}\mathfrak{b}^{2}
. We write
\mathfrak{b}_{F},\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{F}
for the intersection of
\mathfrak{b}
and
\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
with
\mathcal{O}_{F}
. We let
\chi:=\chi_{F}\circ{\rm N}_{E/F}
and we let
f_{E}\in S_{\hat{\kappa}}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}),\chi)
be the unique newform on
E
which generates the automorphic representation of
G
given by the base change of
\pi(f)
. Recall that
\hat{\kappa}=(\hat{\underline{k}},m)
, with
\hat{k}_{\sigma}=\hat{k}_{\sigma^{\prime}}
for every
\sigma\in\Phi
. We also have denoted by
\chi_{0}
the character of
K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})
, which at each finite place
v
is defined by

\chi_{v}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\begin{cases}\chi_{v}(d)&\text{ if }\mathfrak{p}_{v}\mid\mathfrak{c}\\
1&\text{ otherwise,}\end{cases}

with
\mathfrak{p}_{v}
denoting the prime ideal associated with
v
. Poincaré duality gives a perfect pairing

\langle\,,\,\rangle:H^{2d}_{c}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\hat{\underline{k}},-m}(\chi^{-1}_{0}))\times H^{2d}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\hat{\underline{k}},m}(\chi_{0}))\to{\mathbb{C}},

where
H^{2d}_{c}(\bullet)
stands for compactly supported cohomology. Similarly, we have an intersection pairing for the middle intersection cohomology
\langle\,,\,\rangle_{IH}
(with respect to the Baily–Borel compactification of
Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}))
)

\langle\,,\,\rangle_{IH}:{IH}^{2d}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\hat{\underline{k}},-m}(\chi^{-1}_{0}))\times{IH}^{2d}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\hat{\underline{k}},m}(\chi_{0}))\to{\mathbb{C}}.

If we let
\alpha:H^{2d}_{c}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\hat{\underline{k}},-m}(\chi^{-1}_{0}))\to{IH}^{2d}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\hat{\underline{k}},-m}(\chi^{-1}_{0}))
be the composition of the natural morphism from
H^{2d}_{c}(\bullet)
to
L^{2}
-cohomology
H^{2d}_{(2)}(\bullet)
with the isomorphism
\mathcal{Z}
of Zucker’s conjecture (
[
17
, Theorem 4.2]
)

\mathcal{Z}:{H}^{2d}_{(2)}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\hat{\underline{k}},-m}(\chi^{-1}_{0}))\simeq{IH}^{2d}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\hat{\underline{k}},-m}(\chi^{-1}_{0})),

and
\beta
the natural map
{IH}^{2d}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\hat{\underline{k}},m}(\chi_{0}))\to{H}^{2d}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\hat{\underline{k}},m}(\chi_{0}))
, then we
have (
cf
. (3.4.7) of
loc. cit.
)

\langle\omega_{1},\beta(\omega_{2})\rangle=\langle\alpha(\omega_{1}),\omega_{2}\rangle_{IH}.

For any type
J
for
E/F
, the differential form
\omega_{J}(f^{-\iota}_{E})
defines an intersection cohomology class via
\mathcal{Z}
. Let
f_{E}\otimes\theta^{-1}
be the twist of
f_{E}
by
\theta^{-1}
, see
[
17
, Lemma 5.14]
.
By
[
17
, Theorem 10.1]
, there exists an intersection cohomology class

[Z(\mathfrak{c})_{\theta}]\in{IH}^{2d}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\hat{\underline{k}},m}(\chi_{0}))

(made up of a cycle
Z(\mathfrak{c})_{\theta}
, given by
Y_{H}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}_{F}))
twisted by
\theta
and the
H
-invariant section
\Delta^{\hat{\underline{k}}}
of the restricted local system
\mathscr{V}^{\hat{\underline{k}},{m}}_{\mathbb{C}}
) such that

(12)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Z}[\omega_{J}(f^{-\iota}_{E})],[Z(\mathfrak{c})_{\theta}]\rangle_{IH}

\displaystyle=C^{\prime}_{2}\int_{Y_{H}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}_{F}^{\prime}))}\tilde{\iota}^{\hat{\underline{k}},\ast}(\omega_{J}(f^{-\iota}_{E}\otimes\theta^{-1})),

with
5
5

5

Note that our constant is slightly different from the

C^{\prime}_{2}=[K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{F}):\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\ast,+}K_{1}(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{F})][K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}):\mathcal{O}_{E}^{\ast,+}K_{11}(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})]G(\theta^{u})|N_{E/{\mathbb{Q}}}(D_{E/{\mathbb{Q}}})|^{-m/2}

Remark 3.8
.

The pairing can also be calculated with any Hilbert modular form
g
for
E
, or with any subset

of
\Sigma_{E}
, but the resulting integral will vanish if

. Indeed, when the period integral in the right hand side of (

is non-zero. The existence of this pole implies that

(
cf.

[

, Theorem 10.1]
).

Note that the above integral formula can be obtained without using middle intersection cohomology by means of the Hirzebruch–Zagier cycles in the cohomology of the open Hilbert modular variety, as we now explain. Since we ultimately need the following construction for weights
\hat{\kappa}
of the form

.

Let
\mathbf{1}_{K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}_{F})}

and let

Definition 3.9
.

Let

\mathscr{Z}^{\hat{\kappa}}(\mathfrak{c}):=\iota_{\ast}\circ\iota^{\hat{\underline{k}}}(\mathbf{1}_{K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}_{F})})\in{H}^{2d}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\hat{\kappa}}).

Similarly to the definition above, we can define a cycle in the cohomology with coefficients
\mathscr{V}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\hat{\kappa}}(\chi_{0})
. To do so, let

Definition 3.10

This cycle can be further twisted by

{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}1&t_{v}\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}&\text{ if }v\mid\mathfrak{b}.\end{cases}

Consider the correspondence

\textstyle{Y_{G}(K_{11}(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{Y_{G}(K_{11}(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Thus we can give the following (

Definition 3.11
.

Let

\mathscr{Z}^{\hat{\kappa}}(\mathfrak{c})_{\theta}:=\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{b}}(\mathscr{Z}^{\hat{\kappa},\chi\theta^{2}}(\mathfrak{c^{\prime}}))\in{H}^{2d}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c})),\mathscr{V}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\hat{\kappa}}(\chi_{0})).

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
g\in S_{\hat{\kappa}}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}),\chi)

Similarly, if
\theta
is the Hecke character of
{\mathbb{A}}_{E}^{\times}
introduced above,

\langle[\omega_{J}(g^{-\iota})],\mathscr{Z}^{\hat{\kappa}}(\mathfrak{c})_{\theta}\rangle=C_{2}\int_{Y_{H}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}_{F}^{\prime}))}\tilde{\iota}^{\hat{\underline{k}},\ast}(\omega_{J}(g^{-\iota}\otimes\theta^{-1})),

where
C_{2}:=[K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{F}):\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\ast,+}K_{1}(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{F})][K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}):\mathcal{O}_{E}^{\ast,+}K_{11}(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})]G(\theta^{u})
and

\tilde{\iota}^{\hat{\underline{k}},\ast}:H^{2d}(Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})),\mathscr{V}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\hat{\kappa}}(\chi^{-1}_{0}\theta_{0}^{-2}))\to H^{2d}(Y_{H}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}_{F}^{\prime})),{\mathbb{C}})

is the map induced by pullback along
\iota
at the level of varieties and by the

-twisted branching map
\tilde{\iota}^{\hat{\underline{k}}}:{\mathbb{C}}\hookrightarrow\iota^{\ast}\mathscr{V}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\hat{\kappa}}(\chi^{-1}_{0}\theta_{0}^{-2})
as in §
2.4
.

Proof.

For the first part of the statement, we argue exactly as in
[
9
, Proposition 4.8]
. Using Proposition
3.1
, we get that

\displaystyle\langle[\omega_{J}(g^{-\iota})],\mathscr{Z}^{\hat{\kappa}}(\mathfrak{c})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle[\omega_{c}],\mathscr{Z}^{\hat{\kappa}}(\mathfrak{c})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle[\omega_{c}],\iota_{\ast}\circ\iota^{\hat{\underline{k}}}(\mathbf{1}_{K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}_{F})})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\iota^{\hat{\underline{k}},\ast}[\omega_{c}],\mathbf{1}_{K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}_{F})}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{Y_{H}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}_{F}))}\!\!\!\!\iota^{\hat{\underline{k}},\ast}(\omega_{c})

\displaystyle=\int_{Y_{H}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}_{F}))}\!\!\!\!\iota^{\hat{\underline{k}},\ast}(\omega_{J}(g^{-\iota}))+\int_{Y_{H}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}_{F}))}\!\!\!\!\iota^{\hat{\underline{k}},\ast}(d\Psi),

with

a degree

rapidly decreasing form on
Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}))
. To conclude the proof, note that, as explained in
[
6
, §5.6]
,

\int_{Y_{H}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}_{F}))}\!\!\!\!\iota^{\hat{\underline{k}},\ast}(d\Psi)=0.

Regarding the second formula, notice

\displaystyle\langle[\omega_{J}(g^{-\iota})],\mathscr{Z}^{\hat{\kappa}}(\mathfrak{c})_{\theta}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle[\omega_{J}(g^{-\iota})],\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{b}}(\mathscr{Z}^{\hat{\kappa},\chi\theta^{2}}(\mathfrak{c^{\prime}}))\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{b}}^{\prime}[\omega_{J}(g^{-\iota})],\mathscr{Z}^{\hat{\kappa},\chi\theta^{2}}(\mathfrak{c^{\prime}})\rangle

\displaystyle=C_{2}\langle[\omega_{J}(g^{-\iota}\otimes\theta^{-1})],\mathscr{Z}^{\hat{\kappa},\chi\theta^{2}}(\mathfrak{c^{\prime}})\rangle,

where
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{b}}^{\prime}
denotes the adjoint to
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{b}}
and we have used that
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{b}}^{\prime}[\omega_{J}(g^{-\iota})]=C_{2}[\omega_{J}(g^{-\iota}\otimes\theta^{-1})]
(
cf
.
[
17
, Proposition 9.4]
). The commutativity of the diagram

\textstyle{Y_{H}(K_{1}(\mathfrak{c}_{F}^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{P_{10}^{\mathfrak{c}_{F}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{Y_{G}(K_{1}(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P_{10}^{\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{Y_{H}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}_{F}^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{Y_{G}(K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}))}

implies that

\displaystyle P_{10,\ast}^{\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}}\circ\iota_{\ast}(\mathbf{1}_{K_{1}(\mathfrak{c}_{F}^{\prime})})

\displaystyle=\iota_{\ast}\circ P_{10,\ast}^{\mathfrak{c}_{F}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{1}_{K_{1}(\mathfrak{c}_{F}^{\prime})})

\displaystyle=[K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{F}):\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\ast,+}K_{1}(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{F})]\iota_{\ast}(\mathbf{1}_{K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}_{F}^{\prime})}),

with

being the degree of the natural degeneracy map

give that

\displaystyle\langle[\omega_{J}(g^{-\iota}\otimes\theta^{-1})],\mathscr{Z}^{\hat{\kappa},\chi\theta^{2}}(\mathfrak{c^{\prime}})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle[\omega_{J}(g^{-\iota}\otimes\theta^{-1})],P_{10,\ast}^{\chi_{0}\theta^{2}_{0}}\circ\iota_{*}\circ\iota^{\hat{\underline{k}}}(\mathbf{1}_{K_{1}(\mathfrak{c}_{F}^{\prime})})\rangle

\displaystyle=[K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{F}):\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\ast,+}K_{1}(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{F})]\langle\tilde{\iota}^{\hat{\underline{k}},\ast}[\omega_{J}(g^{-\iota}\otimes\theta^{-1})],\mathbf{1}_{K_{0}(\mathfrak{c}_{F}^{\prime})}\rangle.

The result then follows as in the first part of the statement.
∎

Remark 3.13
.

Lemma
3.12
implies in particular that

\langle[\omega_{J}(g^{-\iota})],\mathscr{Z}^{\hat{\kappa}}(\mathfrak{c})_{\theta}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{Z}[\omega_{J}(g^{-\iota})],[Z(\mathfrak{c})_{\theta}]\rangle_{IH}.

Write

\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}:=\prod\{\mathfrak{p}\,:\,\mathfrak{p}\mid\mathfrak{d}_{E/F}\text{ and }\mathfrak{p}\nmid\mathfrak{f}(\theta)\},

with
\mathfrak{f}(\theta)
the conductor of
\theta
. Finally,
[
17
, Theorem 10.1]
reads as follows:

Theorem 3.14
.

For
f
as above, let
\chi_{F}
be a Hecke character for
F
corresponding to the Nebentypus of
f
, and
\theta
a Hecke character for
E
such that
\theta_{|_{{\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\times}}}=\eta_{E/F}\chi_{F}
. Then we have

\langle\omega_{J}(f^{-\iota}_{E}),\mathscr{Z}^{\hat{\kappa}}(\mathfrak{c})_{\theta}\rangle=\frac{c_{1}}{2^{d}}L^{\ast,\mathfrak{d}_{E/F}\mathfrak{b}_{F}}(\mathrm{Ad}(f)\otimes\eta_{E/F},1)\prod_{\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}L_{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{As}(f_{E}\otimes\theta^{-1}),1),

where
L_{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathrm{As}(f_{E}\otimes\theta^{-1}),1)
denotes the Euler factor at
{\mathfrak{q}}
of the Asai
L
-function of
f_{E}
twisted by
\theta^{-1}
and

\displaystyle c_{1}:=\frac{C_{2}\mathrm{N}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{F}}^{2}D_{E/F})D_{F}^{3}\zeta_{F}^{\mathfrak{d}_{E/F}\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{F}}(1)^{*}}{2^{(\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{F}}k_{\sigma})-2}R_{F}|(\mathcal{O}_{F}/\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{F})^{\times}|},

for
R_{F}
the regulator of
F
and
\zeta_{F}^{\mathfrak{d}_{E/F}\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{F}}(1)^{*}
the residue at

of the partial Dedekind zeta function of
F
with the Euler factors at
\mathfrak{d}_{E/F}\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}_{F}
removed.

The proof of the Theorem follows from comparing the left hand side to the residue at

of a Rankin–Selberg integral

which calculates the Asai

-function of

(

.
[

, Chapter 10]
and §

below). Note that the adjoint
